- **Score**: 6/10

### AlignDiff: Learning Physically-Grounded Camera Alignment via Diffusion
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.21581v1)
- **Authors**: Liuyue Xie, Jiancong Guo, Ozan Cakmakci, Andre Araujo, Laszlo A. Jeni, Zhiheng Jia
- **Abstract**: Accurate camera calibration is a fundamental task for 3D perception, especially when dealing with real-world, in-the-wild environments where complex optical distortions are common. Existing methods often rely on pre-rectified images or calibration patterns, which limits their applicability and flexibility. In this work, we introduce a novel framework that addresses these challenges by jointly modeling camera intrinsic and extrinsic parameters using a generic ray camera model. Unlike previous approaches, AlignDiff shifts focus from semantic to geometric features, enabling more accurate modeling of local distortions. We propose AlignDiff, a diffusion model conditioned on geometric priors, enabling the simultaneous estimation of camera distortions and scene geometry. To enhance distortion prediction, we incorporate edge-aware attention, focusing the model on geometric features around image edges, rather than semantic content. Furthermore, to enhance generalizability to real-world captures, we incorporate a large database of ray-traced lenses containing over three thousand samples. This database characterizes the distortion inherent in a diverse variety of lens forms. Our experiments demonstrate that the proposed method significantly reduces the angular error of estimated ray bundles by ~8.2 degrees and overall calibration accuracy, outperforming existing approaches on challenging, real-world datasets.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the "AlignDiff: Learning Physically-Grounded Camera Alignment via Diffusion" paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces AlignDiff, a novel diffusion-based framework for camera calibration that jointly models intrinsic and extrinsic camera parameters.  It addresses the challenges of real-world optical distortions by moving away from relying solely on semantic features and incorporating geometric priors derived from line detection networks.  The framework also integrates edge-aware attention to focus on geometric features around image edges and leverages a large database of ray-traced lenses to enhance generalizability to diverse lens forms. Experiments demonstrate improved accuracy in ray bundle estimation and overall calibration compared to existing methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper presents a few key novel aspects. First, the shift from semantic-driven feature extraction to geometry-driven is a meaningful step, especially for calibration where precise geometric measurements are crucial. Using line embeddings to condition the diffusion model offers a strong focus on structural cues. The incorporation of a large ray-traced lens database to ground the model in physical optical properties is another substantial contribution, addressing a common limitation of simulated distortion models.  While diffusion-based methods for calibration are not entirely new, the specific combination of geometric conditioning, edge-aware attention, and physical lens grounding is a unique contribution. The way they integrate line segments directly into the DiT architecture, and the design of their loss function for denoising are also technically novel.

* **Significance:**  Accurate camera calibration is a fundamental problem in computer vision and robotics. The ability to accurately calibrate cameras in real-world, unconstrained environments with complex optical distortions has significant implications for various applications, including autonomous driving, augmented reality, and 3D reconstruction.  By addressing the limitations of existing methods that rely on pre-rectification or simplified camera models, AlignDiff offers a promising approach for achieving more robust and accurate 3D perception. The gains in angular error are substantial.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Geometric Grounding:**  Shifting the focus to geometric features improves robustness to real-world distortions.
    * **Physical Accuracy:**  Leveraging ray-traced lenses introduces realistic optical profiles.
    * **Joint Optimization:**  Addresses limitations of decoupling intrinsic and extrinsic calibration.
    * **Strong Experimental Results:** The demonstrated improvements over existing methods on challenging datasets indicate its practical value.
    * **Well-written and well-structured.**

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Computational Cost:** Diffusion models are known for their computational demands, potentially limiting real-time applications, though the paper does describe a processing step to keep the model lightweight. More clarification of compute requirements would improve the work.
    * **Scale Ambiguity:** Acknowledged limitation of scale ambiguity in outdoor scenes requires further investigation of integrating scale priors.
    * **Dataset Dependence:** The paper depends heavily on the availability of a very large lens database for achieving generalizability. The creation and curation of this database may be a limitation for other researchers, and should be expanded to more situations for greater generalizability.

* **Potential Influence:** This work has the potential to influence research in camera calibration, 3D reconstruction, and robotics. The geometric conditioning and physical grounding approaches could be adopted and extended by other researchers to improve the accuracy and robustness of their models. The lens dataset could become a valuable resource for the community.

* **Score:** 8

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a novel and technically sound approach to camera calibration that addresses critical limitations of existing methods. The integration of geometric conditioning, edge-aware attention, and physical lens grounding is a significant contribution that leads to demonstrably improved results. The potential impact of this work is substantial, and it is likely to inspire further research in this area. While the computational cost and potential scale ambiguities represent limitations, the overall strengths of the paper outweigh these weaknesses. As a result, it scores a solid 8.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### Critical Iterative Denoising: A Discrete Generative Model Applied to Graphs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.21592v1)
- **Authors**: Yoann Boget, Alexandros Kalousis
- **Abstract**: Discrete Diffusion and Flow Matching models have significantly advanced generative modeling for discrete structures, including graphs. However, the time dependencies in the noising process of these models lead to error accumulation and propagation during the backward process. This issue, particularly pronounced in mask diffusion, is a known limitation in sequence modeling and, as we demonstrate, also impacts discrete diffusion models for graphs. To address this problem, we propose a novel framework called Iterative Denoising, which simplifies discrete diffusion and circumvents the issue by assuming conditional independence across time. Additionally, we enhance our model by incorporating a Critic, which during generation selectively retains or corrupts elements in an instance based on their likelihood under the data distribution. Our empirical evaluations demonstrate that the proposed method significantly outperforms existing discrete diffusion baselines in graph generation tasks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "Critical Iterative Denoising" (CID), a novel generative modeling framework for discrete structures, particularly graphs.  CID addresses limitations in existing Discrete Diffusion and Flow Matching models, which suffer from error accumulation due to time dependencies in their noising processes. CID simplifies discrete diffusion by assuming conditional independence across time, effectively circumventing the error propagation issue.  It also incorporates a "Critic" that selectively retains or corrupts elements during generation based on their likelihood under the data distribution, guided by a GNN. Empirically, the authors demonstrate that CID outperforms existing discrete diffusion baselines in graph generation tasks, particularly in generating valid molecular structures. The approach doesn't rely on complex corrector sampling methods, and in the experiments, CID shows its efficiency by reaching almost 100% molecule validity with only a few denoising steps.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in its simplification of the discrete diffusion process by assuming conditional independence across time (Iterative Denoising) and the introduction of a Critic network to guide the denoising process. The concept of conditional independence significantly simplifies the denoising process and distinguishes it from standard diffusion models. Also, the application of a Critic to modulate the corruption probability based on data likelihood is a creative enhancement.

*   **Significance:** The significance stems from addressing a crucial limitation of diffusion models: error accumulation. By mitigating this issue, the method improves generative performance, especially for tasks demanding high validity, like molecular graph generation. The empirical results, demonstrating superior performance and efficiency, are compelling. The high validity rates, particularly on the challenging Zinc250k dataset, suggest a practical contribution.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper clearly identifies and articulates the problem of error accumulation in existing discrete diffusion models.
    *   The proposed solution, Iterative Denoising with a Critic, is well-motivated and explained.
    *   The experimental evaluation is comprehensive, covering molecular and synthetic graph datasets and comparing against strong baselines.
    *   Ablation studies convincingly demonstrate the effectiveness of the Iterative Denoising and the Critic components.
    *   The analysis is relatively complete (theoretical results, discussion).

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   While the assumption of conditional independence simplifies the process, it might limit the model's ability to capture more complex dependencies present in some datasets. The paper acknowledges this in some way by claiming the results would be less localized for general graphs. Further investigation is needed regarding its limitations.
    *   The theoretical analysis of the optimal Critic is present, but the design choices of the Critic GNN architecture could benefit from more in-depth justification/discussion. The GNN might introduce its own bias, and how to best train/integrate it requires further consideration.
    *   The paper focuses primarily on graphs. While the Iterative Denoising framework is intended to be generally applicable to discrete data, further evidence of its effectiveness in other domains would strengthen the claim of broad applicability. The mention of sequence modeling is not sufficient to evaluate this.
    *   Some results are taken directly from previous work, limiting the scope.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field by offering a more efficient and effective approach to discrete generative modeling. The simplification of diffusion through conditional independence could inspire further research in this direction. The introduction of the Critic demonstrates the utility of incorporating domain knowledge to guide the generative process. The demonstrated performance on molecular graph generation is also valuable for drug discovery and material design.

**Rigorous Rationale for the Score:**

The paper offers a clear, well-motivated solution to a significant problem in discrete diffusion modeling. The approach demonstrates strong empirical results, providing evidence of its practical value. However, the limitations of the conditional independence assumption and the need for broader evaluation across different discrete data domains temper the overall impact. While the integration of the Critic is novel, more theoretical justification would be beneficial.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Prompt, Divide, and Conquer: Bypassing Large Language Model Safety Filters via Segmented and Distributed Prompt Processing
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.21598v1)
- **Authors**: Johan Wahréus, Ahmed Hussain, Panos Papadimitratos
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have transformed task automation and content generation across various domains while incorporating safety filters to prevent misuse. We introduce a novel jailbreaking framework that employs distributed prompt processing combined with iterative refinements to bypass these safety measures, particularly in generating malicious code. Our architecture consists of four key modules: prompt segmentation, parallel processing, response aggregation, and LLM-based jury evaluation. Tested on 500 malicious prompts across 10 cybersecurity categories, the framework achieves a 73.2% Success Rate (SR) in generating malicious code. Notably, our comparative analysis reveals that traditional single-LLM judge evaluation overestimates SRs (93.8%) compared to our LLM jury system (73.2%), with manual verification confirming that single-judge assessments often accept incomplete implementations. Moreover, we demonstrate that our distributed architecture improves SRs by 12% over the non-distributed approach in an ablation study, highlighting both the effectiveness of distributed prompt processing and the importance of robust evaluation methodologies in assessing jailbreak attempts.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Prompt, Divide, and Conquer: Bypassing Large Language Model Safety Filters via Segmented and Distributed Prompt Processing":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel framework to jailbreak Large Language Models (LLMs) by using segmented and distributed prompt processing.  Instead of sending a single, potentially detectable malicious prompt to an LLM, the framework decomposes the prompt into smaller, seemingly benign segments. These segments are processed in parallel across a network of LLMs, and the responses are then aggregated and refined.  An LLM-based jury system is used for evaluation. The authors demonstrate that this approach can significantly improve the success rate of generating malicious code compared to traditional single-prompt jailbreaking methods, and that the LLM-based jury provides a more robust evaluation than a single-LLM judge. They test the framework on the CySecBench dataset and perform an ablation study to highlight the benefits of the distributed architecture.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of segmenting and distributing prompts for jailbreaking is relatively novel. While mosaic prompting exists, this paper introduces a systematic framework with parallel processing and iterative refinements, which represents a significant step forward. The use of an LLM jury system to evaluate the effectiveness of jailbreaking attempts is also a valuable contribution, addressing the documented issue of self-bias in single-LLM evaluations.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance lies in highlighting the weaknesses of current LLM safety filters and demonstrating a potentially more effective method for bypassing them. This is a crucial area of research, given the increasing use of LLMs and the potential for malicious actors to exploit them. The findings underscore the need for more robust safety mechanisms and evaluation methodologies. However, the paper primarily focuses on malicious code generation, limiting the generalizability of the findings to other types of LLM misuse. The evaluation on a specialized dataset like CySecBench, while valuable, may not fully reflect the framework's performance on other domains.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The distributed prompt processing approach is well-motivated and logically sound.
    *   The architecture of the framework is clearly explained, with a detailed breakdown of each module.
    *   The empirical evaluation is comprehensive, using a relevant dataset and including an ablation study.
    *   The inclusion of an LLM jury system addresses an important limitation of previous evaluation methods.
    *   The paper provides a valuable discussion of the implications and limitations of the research.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The framework relies on access to multiple LLMs, which may not be feasible for all users. This has an implication for the replicability and accessibility of the presented methodology.
    *   The evaluation is primarily focused on generating malicious code. While significant, this is only one aspect of potential LLM misuse.
    *   The LLM Jury is itself vulnerable to bias and hallucination, though the ensemble approach mitigates this, it may still affect the results.
    *   The reliance on closed-source proprietary LLMs presents a hurdle for reproducibility and open research.
    *   While the paper touches on ethical considerations, a more in-depth discussion of the potential societal impact of this research would be beneficial.
    *   Although comparisons are made, the work relies heavily on results generated by other LLMs, which are frequently updated and change in effectiveness.

*   **Potential Influence:** This paper could influence future research in several ways:
    *   It could inspire the development of more sophisticated jailbreaking techniques.
    *   It could motivate the creation of more robust and diverse LLM safety filters.
    *   It could lead to the development of better evaluation methodologies for assessing LLM safety.
    *   The LLM jury concept could be adopted in other areas of AI evaluation.

**Score:** 7

**Justification:**

The paper presents a reasonably novel and significant approach to jailbreaking LLMs, addressing a critical security concern. The thorough evaluation and the introduction of the LLM jury system are valuable contributions. However, the reliance on multiple LLMs, limited scope (malicious code generation), and the inherent limitations of LLM-based evaluations reduce the paper's overall impact and generalizability. The ethical implications, while acknowledged, could be more thoroughly addressed. Despite these limitations, the paper provides important insights and could influence future research directions in LLM security. As such, it warrants a score of 7, indicating a solid contribution with some limitations in scope and generalizability.

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 7/10

### GenEdit: Compounding Operators and Continuous Improvement to Tackle Text-to-SQL in the Enterprise
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.21602v1)
- **Authors**: Karime Maamari, Connor Landy, Amine Mhedhbi
- **Abstract**: Recent advancements in Text-to-SQL, driven by large language models, are democratizing data access. Despite these advancements, enterprise deployments remain challenging due to the need to capture business-specific knowledge, handle complex queries, and meet expectations of continuous improvements. To address these issues, we designed and implemented GenEdit: our Text-to-SQL generation system that improves with user feedback. GenEdit builds and maintains a company-specific knowledge set, employs a pipeline of operators decomposing SQL generation, and uses feedback to update its knowledge set to improve future SQL generations. We describe GenEdit's architecture made of two core modules: (i) decomposed SQL generation; and (ii) knowledge set edits based on user feedback. For generation, GenEdit leverages compounding operators to improve knowledge retrieval and to create a plan as chain-of-thought steps that guides generation. GenEdit first retrieves relevant examples in an initial retrieval stage where original SQL queries are decomposed into sub-statements, clauses or sub-queries. It then also retrieves instructions and schema elements. Using the retrieved contextual information, GenEdit then generates step-by-step plan in natural language on how to produce the query. Finally, GenEdit uses the plan to generate SQL, minimizing the need for model reasoning, which enhances complex SQL generation. If necessary, GenEdit regenerates the query based on syntactic and semantic errors. The knowledge set edits are recommended through an interactive copilot, allowing users to iterate on their feedback and to regenerate SQL queries as needed. Each generation uses staged edits which update the generation prompt. Once the feedback is submitted, it gets merged after passing regression testing and obtaining an approval, improving future generations.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces GenEdit, a Text-to-SQL system designed for enterprise environments. It addresses challenges like capturing company-specific knowledge, handling complex queries, and enabling continuous improvement. GenEdit employs a pipeline of operators that decompose SQL generation, leveraging a company-specific knowledge set that is updated with user feedback. The system includes two core modules: decomposed SQL generation (which uses compounding operators) and knowledge set edits based on user feedback. It generates a chain-of-thought plan to guide SQL generation and relies on interactive copilots to recommend edits and manage user feedback.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in several aspects:
    *   **Compounding operators:** The way that operators are designed such that outputs of prior operators inform the retrieval and ranking of information by subsequent operators.
    *   **Decomposed examples:** Instead of few-shot examples as query-SQL pairs, the SQL queries are decomposed into smaller clauses, sub-queries or sub-statements.
    *   **Interactive Collaboration:** In analytics engine, users can provide feedback through a user interface to regenerate the query. Feedback generates the recommendations of edits to the knowledge set.

    The use of CoT planning to minimize reliance on LLM reasoning is also a valuable contribution. The end-to-end system design is enterprise focused and designed to meet real-world deployment requirements and continuous improvement.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance stems from its potential to improve the practical applicability of Text-to-SQL systems in enterprise settings. Many existing Text-to-SQL solutions struggle with complex queries and domain-specific knowledge. GenEdit addresses these issues with a combination of decomposed SQL generation, knowledge set management, and user feedback integration. The approach has been proven to be better than previous state-of-the-art solutions, although the newer benchmakrs are not used.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper tackles a relevant and important problem: making Text-to-SQL practical for real-world enterprise use cases.
    *   The architecture is well-defined and the components (compounding operators, planning for generation, decomposed examples, etc.) are clearly explained.
    *   The interactive collaboration aspect with user feedback is crucial for continuous improvement and adaptation to specific business needs.
    *   The system has been deployed in practice.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The evaluation is limited to the BIRD benchmark's dev set, which could be expanded. It has not been tested on more modern benchmarks.
    *   While comparisons are made to other Text-to-SQL methods, more detailed comparisons and ablation studies could strengthen the analysis.
    *   The paper could benefit from more discussion of the challenges and limitations of the approach, particularly in terms of scalability, knowledge set maintenance, and the potential for feedback bias.
    *   The evaluation metric relies on execution accuracy which has limitations in enterprise settings. It could be argued that incorrect answers can lead to financial risk, but execution can be complex and difficult to diagnose for some cases.

**Overall:**
The paper presents a valuable contribution to the field of Text-to-SQL, with a strong focus on practical applicability in enterprise environments. The novelty of some design decisions, the clear architecture, and the focus on user feedback make it a promising approach.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:** The paper addresses a critical gap in the Text-to-SQL field by focusing on enterprise deployment challenges. While not revolutionary in terms of fundamental technical breakthroughs, the integrated system design, focus on user feedback, and enterprise-specific knowledge make it a significant and practical contribution. The limitations in the evaluation data set, more comprehensive comparisons, and deeper discussion of challenges bring the score down, but the practical relevance and promising results justify a relatively high score.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### Evaluating book summaries from internal knowledge in Large Language Models: a cross-model and semantic consistency approach
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.21613v1)
- **Authors**: Javier Coronado-Blázquez
- **Abstract**: We study the ability of large language models (LLMs) to generate comprehensive and accurate book summaries solely from their internal knowledge, without recourse to the original text. Employing a diverse set of books and multiple LLM architectures, we examine whether these models can synthesize meaningful narratives that align with established human interpretations. Evaluation is performed with a LLM-as-a-judge paradigm: each AI-generated summary is compared against a high-quality, human-written summary via a cross-model assessment, where all participating LLMs evaluate not only their own outputs but also those produced by others. This methodology enables the identification of potential biases, such as the proclivity for models to favor their own summarization style over others. In addition, alignment between the human-crafted and LLM-generated summaries is quantified using ROUGE and BERTScore metrics, assessing the depth of grammatical and semantic correspondence. The results reveal nuanced variations in content representation and stylistic preferences among the models, highlighting both strengths and limitations inherent in relying on internal knowledge for summarization tasks. These findings contribute to a deeper understanding of LLM internal encodings of factual information and the dynamics of cross-model evaluation, with implications for the development of more robust natural language generative systems.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the ability of Large Language Models (LLMs) to generate summaries of well-known books based solely on their internal knowledge acquired during training.  It prompts several LLMs to summarize 50 different books without providing external access to the text.  The generated summaries are then evaluated against human-written summaries using both automated metrics (ROUGE, BERTScore) and a novel "LLM-as-a-judge" paradigm, where LLMs assess each other's summaries.  The study aims to understand how factual information is encoded and retrieved by LLMs, and to identify potential biases in cross-model evaluation.  The results indicate that while LLMs possess internal knowledge sufficient to generate summaries, the quality is often mediocre, with hallucinations, factual errors, and inconsistencies. Furthermore, standard summarization metrics are shown to be insufficient for this type of evaluation, and cross-model evaluation doesn't always eliminate self-preference bias.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The "LLM-as-a-judge" aspect is the most novel component. While using LLMs to evaluate other LLMs is becoming increasingly common, the rigorous cross-evaluation setup, aiming to mitigate self-preference bias, is a valuable contribution. Asking models to summarize from internal knowledge, though not entirely new, provides a specific context to assess the effectiveness of the internal representation of factual information in LLMs.
*   **Significance:** The work addresses a crucial aspect of LLM capabilities: the quality and reliability of knowledge stored within the model's parameters. Understanding how LLMs acquire, store, and retrieve factual information is essential for building more trustworthy and controllable AI systems. Revealing the limitations of summarization based solely on internal knowledge highlights the need for improved knowledge encoding and retrieval mechanisms.  The study's analysis of evaluation biases is also significant for the broader field of LLM benchmarking.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   Comprehensive experimental setup: The paper uses a diverse set of books and multiple LLM architectures, providing a broad perspective.
    *   Introduction and exploration of LLM-as-a-judge with modifications to prevent self-preference.
    *   Detailed analysis of results: The paper examines not only aggregate scores but also individual book performance and inter-model variations.
    *   Clear presentation and well-structured writing.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The number of LLMs and books tested could be seen as a limitation.
    *   The use of only one set of human-written summaries (from SparkNotes) might introduce bias, as they may not be the ideal "ground truth."
    *   While the LLM-as-a-judge paradigm is interesting, it still relies on LLMs that might have their own biases and limitations. It would be beneficial to compare those results to a few human evaluators.
    *   The conclusions are somewhat negative, showing limitations of LLMs.  The work could be strengthened by proposing specific avenues for improvement based on the identified weaknesses.

*   **Impact:** The paper's insights on the limitations of internal knowledge summarization and the importance of careful evaluation methodologies could influence future research directions.  The findings could encourage the development of more robust knowledge integration techniques for LLMs and more reliable evaluation frameworks.

**Rigorous Rationale for Score:**

The paper presents a well-executed study that provides valuable insights into the capabilities and limitations of LLMs in a specific, but important, task. The cross-model evaluation is a novel approach to benchmark LLMs. While it has limitations, this approach adds a unique perspective to the ongoing discussion about LLM evaluation and knowledge representation. The paper's strengths are primarily methodological, but the findings, while not groundbreaking, are informative and contribute to a better understanding of how LLMs handle factual knowledge.
It's not a *transformative* paper that will revolutionize the field, but it's a solid contribution with a useful methodology and interesting observations.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### A Survey of Efficient Reasoning for Large Reasoning Models: Language, Multimodality, and Beyond
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.21614v1)
- **Authors**: Xiaoye Qu, Yafu Li, Zhaochen Su, Weigao Sun, Jianhao Yan, Dongrui Liu, Ganqu Cui, Daizong Liu, Shuxian Liang, Junxian He, Peng Li, Wei Wei, Jing Shao, Chaochao Lu, Yue Zhang, Xian-Sheng Hua, Bowen Zhou, Yu Cheng
- **Abstract**: Recent Large Reasoning Models (LRMs), such as DeepSeek-R1 and OpenAI o1, have demonstrated strong performance gains by scaling up the length of Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning during inference. However, a growing concern lies in their tendency to produce excessively long reasoning traces, which are often filled with redundant content (e.g., repeated definitions), over-analysis of simple problems, and superficial exploration of multiple reasoning paths for harder tasks. This inefficiency introduces significant challenges for training, inference, and real-world deployment (e.g., in agent-based systems), where token economy is critical. In this survey, we provide a comprehensive overview of recent efforts aimed at improving reasoning efficiency in LRMs, with a particular focus on the unique challenges that arise in this new paradigm. We identify common patterns of inefficiency, examine methods proposed across the LRM lifecycle, i.e., from pretraining to inference, and discuss promising future directions for research. To support ongoing development, we also maintain a real-time GitHub repository tracking recent progress in the field. We hope this survey serves as a foundation for further exploration and inspires innovation in this rapidly evolving area.
- **Summary**: This paper surveys recent efforts to improve reasoning efficiency in Large Reasoning Models (LRMs). It defines reasoning efficiency as a trade-off between solution quality and computational cost, and identifies common patterns of inefficiency such as redundant content, overthinking simple questions, and incoherent reasoning. The survey comprehensively reviews methods across the LRM lifecycle, from pre-training to inference, including length budgeting, system/model switching, parallel search, supervised fine-tuning (SFT), and reinforcement learning (RL). It also discusses efficient model structures and promising future research directions, while maintaining a real-time GitHub repository to track progress.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper provides a valuable and timely overview of an emerging area within LLMs: reasoning efficiency. Its key strengths lie in its comprehensive scope, well-defined terminology, and systematic categorization of existing approaches. The paper clearly articulates the problem of inefficient reasoning, provides a formal definition of reasoning efficiency, and organizes various methods according to the stages of the LRM lifecycle (pre-training, SFT, RL, and inference).

However, the paper has some limitations. First, while the survey is comprehensive, it primarily focuses on summarizing existing methods rather than providing deep analytical insights or comparative analyses across different techniques. The paper could be significantly strengthened by including quantitative or qualitative comparisons of the effectiveness and limitations of different approaches. Second, the identification of "future directions" tends to be broad and general, lacking concrete and actionable suggestions for researchers. For example, deeper explorations of the challenges inherent in each future direction might make this section more valuable. Third, while the GitHub repository is a useful resource, its impact on the field would be more significant if the survey included structured benchmarks or evaluation metrics to facilitate comparison of different reasoning efficiency methods. Finally, the paper leans heavily towards surveying the literature without offering significant original perspectives. Although a survey, it could benefit from more critical analyses of the surveyed works.

Despite these limitations, the paper addresses a critical and rapidly evolving area of research. The taxonomy of techniques and the organization of content are very useful for newcomers and seasoned researchers alike. By highlighting the problem of reasoning inefficiency, the paper serves as a valuable contribution that can guide future research efforts in LRM optimization. The paper's significance stems from its ability to synthesize a fragmented body of research and provide a coherent framework for understanding and addressing the challenges of efficient reasoning in LRMs. Given its contributions and identified limitations, a reasonable score is:

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Audio-driven Gesture Generation via Deviation Feature in the Latent Space
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.21616v1)
- **Authors**: Jiahui Chen, Yang Huan, Runhua Shi, Chanfan Ding, Xiaoqi Mo, Siyu Xiong, Yinong He
- **Abstract**: Gestures are essential for enhancing co-speech communication, offering visual emphasis and complementing verbal interactions. While prior work has concentrated on point-level motion or fully supervised data-driven methods, we focus on co-speech gestures, advocating for weakly supervised learning and pixel-level motion deviations. We introduce a weakly supervised framework that learns latent representation deviations, tailored for co-speech gesture video generation. Our approach employs a diffusion model to integrate latent motion features, enabling more precise and nuanced gesture representation. By leveraging weakly supervised deviations in latent space, we effectively generate hand gestures and mouth movements, crucial for realistic video production. Experiments show our method significantly improves video quality, surpassing current state-of-the-art techniques.
- **Summary**: Okay, I've reviewed the paper "Audio-driven Gesture Generation via Deviation Feature in the Latent Space." Here's a summary and a critical evaluation:

**Summary:**

The paper proposes a new approach for generating co-speech gesture videos. It focuses on learning motion deviations in the latent space using a weakly supervised framework. The key components are a deviation module (latent deviation extractor, warping calculator, latent deviation decoder) and a latent motion diffusion model.  The method leverages a combination of weakly supervised learning for motion representation and a diffusion model to generate coherent and realistic gestures. Experiments show improved video quality compared to state-of-the-art techniques.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Novelty:** The core idea of learning motion *deviations* in the latent space is interesting. It moves away from directly predicting motion or using fully supervised methods, which can be expensive and less flexible. The deviation module, particularly the Latent Deviation Extractor (LDE), represents a solid architectural contribution.
    *   **Weakly Supervised Learning:** The use of a weakly supervised strategy is a significant advantage. It reduces the need for extensive manual annotations, a major bottleneck in this area. The design of losses to support this strategy is well thought out.
    *   **Integration with Diffusion Models:** Using diffusion models for video generation is a popular trend, and this paper integrates it effectively with the deviation learning framework. This allows for more realistic and diverse gesture generation.
    *   **Quantitative Results:** The experimental results (FGD, FVD, BAS, MOS) generally demonstrate improvements over existing methods. The ablation study provides evidence for the importance of the different components.
    *   **Qualitative Results:** The visual comparisons show that the generated videos have fewer artifacts (jitter, blurring) and more natural gestures compared to the baselines. The handling of facial expressions and lip movements also appears improved.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Dataset:** The paper uses a limited dataset of 4 individuals for training. This can limit the generalization of the model to unseen speakers or gesture styles. While using existing datasets is common, acknowledging this limitation and perhaps including results with a more diverse dataset would strengthen the paper.
    *   **Incremental Improvement:** While the results are positive, the improvements over SOTA may not be considered drastic in some metrics. A more in-depth analysis or justification for the design choices leading to the observed improvements would be beneficial.
    *   **Clarity of Writing:** Some sections (especially the loss functions and implementation details) are a bit dense and could be made more accessible. The description of the warping calculator and its integration with the feature maps could be more detailed and explained more visually.
    *   **Limited Ablation Study:** While present, a more granular ablation study could be conducted. For instance, it would be useful to analyze the performance of the model with different noise levels in latent space of the diffusion model, or explore alternative configurations of the latent deviation module.

*   **Significance:**

    The paper addresses a relevant and challenging problem with practical applications in virtual avatars, human-computer interaction, and video synthesis. The proposed method offers a viable alternative to existing approaches, especially in scenarios where large annotated datasets are unavailable. The focus on motion deviations and the weakly supervised learning strategy could influence future research in this area. The combination with latent diffusion models is relevant to the current state of research.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper presents a novel and well-executed approach to a challenging problem. The use of weakly supervised deviation learning, combined with a diffusion model, offers a promising direction for gesture generation. While the improvements are incremental and the dataset is limited, the core idea and the experimental results justify a positive evaluation. Further, the potential practical impact and the approach to weakly supervised learning make it a significant contribution.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### UI-R1: Enhancing Action Prediction of GUI Agents by Reinforcement Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.21620v1)
- **Authors**: Zhengxi Lu, Yuxiang Chai, Yaxuan Guo, Xi Yin, Liang Liu, Hao Wang, Guanjing Xiong, Hongsheng Li
- **Abstract**: The recent DeepSeek-R1 has showcased the emergence of reasoning capabilities in LLMs through reinforcement learning (RL) with rule-based rewards. Building on this idea, we are the first to explore how rule-based RL can enhance the reasoning capabilities of multimodal large language models (MLLMs) for graphic user interface (GUI) action prediction tasks. To this end, we curate a small yet high-quality dataset of 136 challenging tasks, encompassing five common action types on mobile devices. We also introduce a unified rule-based action reward, enabling model optimization via policy-based algorithms such as Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO). Experimental results demonstrate that our proposed data-efficient model, UI-R1-3B, achieves substantial improvements on both in-domain (ID) and out-of-domain (OOD) tasks. Specifically, on the ID benchmark AndroidControl, the action type accuracy improves by 15%, while grounding accuracy increases by 10.3%, compared with the base model (i.e. Qwen2.5-VL-3B). On the OOD GUI grounding benchmark ScreenSpot-Pro, our model surpasses the base model by 6.0% and achieves competitive performance with larger models (e.g., OS-Atlas-7B), which are trained via supervised fine-tuning (SFT) on 76K data. These results underscore the potential of rule-based reinforcement learning to advance GUI understanding and control, paving the way for future research in this domain.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the UI-R1 paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces UI-R1, a reinforcement learning (RL) framework designed to enhance the action prediction capabilities of multimodal large language models (MLLMs) when interacting with graphic user interfaces (GUIs). The authors address the limitations of supervised fine-tuning (SFT), which requires large labeled datasets and struggles with out-of-domain (OOD) generalization.  UI-R1 employs rule-based RL with a unified action reward function to improve reasoning on GUI tasks. The authors curate a small, high-quality dataset of GUI tasks for mobile devices.  Experiments demonstrate that a 3B parameter UI-R1 model achieves substantial improvements in both in-domain and OOD performance compared to a base model and even rivals larger SFT-trained models.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The key novelty lies in the application of rule-based RL to GUI action prediction specifically using a reward function tailored to GUI actions (action type and click coordinates).  Prior work has explored RL in other areas, including mathematical reasoning and vision-related tasks like image grounding. The GUI domain presents unique challenges related to reasoning about interface elements and hierarchical actions. Also novel is the targeted and carefully selected dataset of difficult examples, allowing the reinforcement learning process to focus on the most critical areas.

* **Significance:** The significance stems from the potential to reduce the reliance on large, manually labeled datasets for training GUI agents. SFT is known to be data-hungry and domain-specific. The ability to train performant GUI agents with a significantly smaller, carefully curated dataset is important for several reasons: (1) it reduces the cost and effort associated with data collection; (2) it potentially allows for more rapid adaptation to new GUI domains; (3) the increase in OOD performance hints that the model is learning something more abstract than a particular task. The paper highlights the potential of RL to enhance GUI understanding and control. The competitive performance compared to much larger SFT models on OOD benchmarks strengthens this claim.

* **Strengths:**
    * The core idea of adapting rule-based RL to the GUI domain with a dedicated reward function is well-motivated.
    * The creation of a small, high-quality dataset through a principled selection process (quality, difficulty, diversity) is a valuable contribution, especially when compared to solely relying on raw data quantity.
    * The experimental results are compelling, demonstrating improvements in both in-domain and out-of-domain tasks compared to baseline models.
    * Ablation studies are included that justify design decisions in the study.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * The dataset size, while intentionally small, is still a limitation. Further experiments with a larger and more diverse dataset are needed to fully assess the scalability and robustness of the approach. While there is an ablation on data selection, the actual quantity of selected data remains low.
    * The complexity of the GUI tasks considered may not fully represent the wide range of real-world GUI interactions. Most notably, the action space is fairly limited.
    * While the results show significant improvements, there is a limited analysis of the *types* of errors the model still makes or where it is most frequently struggling. A qualitative error analysis could provide more insight for future improvements.
    * The reward function seems somewhat simplistic. More sophisticated reward shaping techniques (perhaps incorporating elements of task completion or efficiency) could potentially lead to further gains.
    * There is minimal consideration of the potential for negative side effects from reinforcement learning, such as reward hacking or unintended behavior. While unlikely to be a problem with the given reward and task setting, such problems could arise with greater complexity.

* **Potential Influence:**  The paper could influence the development of more data-efficient and adaptable GUI agents. It provides a concrete example of how RL can be applied to this domain, inspiring future research on more sophisticated reward functions, RL algorithms, and transfer learning techniques. Also, it will prompt further research into data curation and selection for reinforcement learning.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:** The paper presents a solid contribution to the field by demonstrating the effectiveness of rule-based RL for GUI action prediction with a novel reward function and data selection strategy.  The data efficiency shown is promising.  The paper is well-written and the experiments are generally well-designed. However, the relatively small dataset size and the limited complexity of the tasks leave room for improvement and further investigation.  The paper offers a clear path forward for more advanced GUI agents that require less data and generalize better. This is more than an incremental improvement and suggests promise.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### Intelligent IoT Attack Detection Design via ODLLM with Feature Ranking-based Knowledge Base
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.21674v1)
- **Authors**: Satvik Verma, Qun Wang, E. Wes Bethel
- **Abstract**: The widespread adoption of Internet of Things (IoT) devices has introduced significant cybersecurity challenges, particularly with the increasing frequency and sophistication of Distributed Denial of Service (DDoS) attacks. Traditional machine learning (ML) techniques often fall short in detecting such attacks due to the complexity of blended and evolving patterns. To address this, we propose a novel framework leveraging On-Device Large Language Models (ODLLMs) augmented with fine-tuning and knowledge base (KB) integration for intelligent IoT network attack detection. By implementing feature ranking techniques and constructing both long and short KBs tailored to model capacities, the proposed framework ensures efficient and accurate detection of DDoS attacks while overcoming computational and privacy limitations. Simulation results demonstrate that the optimized framework achieves superior accuracy across diverse attack types, especially when using compact models in edge computing environments. This work provides a scalable and secure solution for real-time IoT security, advancing the applicability of edge intelligence in cybersecurity.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper proposes a novel framework for detecting Distributed Denial of Service (DDoS) attacks in Internet of Things (IoT) environments. The core idea is to leverage On-Device Large Language Models (ODLLMs) augmented with a knowledge base (KB) built using feature ranking techniques.  The authors use a Random Forest Regressor (RFR) to rank features based on their importance for different DDoS attack types. This ranking informs the construction of both long and short KBs, tailored to the capacities of different ODLLM models. The system aims to improve detection accuracy while addressing computational resource constraints and privacy concerns in edge computing scenarios. Experiments on the CICIOT 2023 dataset demonstrate the effectiveness of the framework, particularly with the use of compact models and tailored KBs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The paper presents an interesting combination of existing techniques, but the overall novelty is somewhat limited.  The use of machine learning for DDoS detection is well-established.  Similarly, the application of LLMs (even on-device) to network security is gaining traction.  The specific contributions, therefore, lie in the *combination* of these elements and the engineering of the knowledge base.

    *   The feature ranking using RFR is a standard technique, and while applied to a specific dataset, doesn't present a fundamentally new approach.
    *   The construction of long and short KBs to adapt to different model sizes is a practical engineering consideration, but not a groundbreaking innovation.

    *   The idea of integrating a KB with an ODLLM to improve attack detection accuracy on edge devices is **somewhat novel**. The paper adequately implements this idea.

*   **Significance:** The significance of the paper stems from its practical focus on resource-constrained IoT environments. Edge intelligence is crucial for real-time security, and the ability to run attack detection models on-device has clear advantages in terms of latency and privacy.

    *   The experimental results, demonstrating improved accuracy with tailored KBs, support the claim that the proposed framework can enhance the performance of ODLLMs in DDoS detection. However, more comparative experiments with other competitive methods would strengthen the work.

    *   The work could be more significant if it tackled more challenging and timely issues, such as addressing adversarial attacks against the ODLLMs themselves or providing stronger privacy guarantees.
    *   The paper provides a clear direction on how to create a KB for ODLLM-based intrusion detection systems in edge devices.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   The paper addresses a relevant and important problem: securing IoT devices against DDoS attacks.
    *   The use of ODLLMs is well-motivated, given the need for low-latency and privacy-preserving solutions.
    *   The feature ranking and KB construction methodology is clearly explained.
    *   The experimental results provide evidence of the effectiveness of the approach.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The individual techniques (RFR, KB, LLMs) are not particularly novel in themselves.
    *   The experimental evaluation could be more comprehensive. It would benefit from comparisons with other state-of-the-art DDoS detection methods, especially those designed for edge environments. A comparison to more traditional ML methods would be helpful.
    *   The paper lacks a detailed analysis of the computational overhead of the proposed framework, which is crucial for edge deployment. More details on the model size and inference time for each KB/model combination are needed.
    *   The security aspects of the proposed framework should be thoroughly investigated, especially concerning model poisoning and adversarial attacks.
    *   The analysis of edge deployment limitations (power, connectivity) could be expanded.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper could influence the development of more efficient and privacy-aware DDoS detection systems for IoT devices.  It provides a practical example of how to combine ODLLMs with knowledge bases for network security tasks. However, its influence will depend on whether the proposed framework can be scaled and adapted to real-world deployments.

**Justification of Score:**

Considering the limited novelty of the individual techniques, the lack of extensive experimental comparison, and the absence of detailed analysis of security aspects, the paper falls short of being a truly groundbreaking contribution. However, the practical focus on ODLLMs in edge environments and the experimental results demonstrating improved accuracy with tailored KBs warrant a moderate score.

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 6/10

### How do language models learn facts? Dynamics, curricula and hallucinations
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.21676v1)
- **Authors**: Nicolas Zucchet, Jörg Bornschein, Stephanie Chan, Andrew Lampinen, Razvan Pascanu, Soham De
- **Abstract**: Large language models accumulate vast knowledge during pre-training, yet the dynamics governing this acquisition remain poorly understood. This work investigates the learning dynamics of language models on a synthetic factual recall task, uncovering three key findings: First, language models learn in three phases, exhibiting a performance plateau before acquiring precise factual knowledge. Mechanistically, this plateau coincides with the formation of attention-based circuits that support recall. Second, the training data distribution significantly impacts learning dynamics, as imbalanced distributions lead to shorter plateaus. Finally, hallucinations emerge simultaneously with knowledge, and integrating new knowledge into the model through fine-tuning is challenging, as it quickly corrupts its existing parametric memories. Our results emphasize the importance of data distribution in knowledge acquisition and suggest novel data scheduling strategies to accelerate neural network training.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary and a rigorous, critical evaluation of the paper "How do language models learn facts? Dynamics, curricula and hallucinations," including a novelty/significance score with justification.

**Summary**

The paper investigates the learning dynamics of language models (LMs) on a synthetic factual recall task. The authors identify three key phases in the knowledge acquisition process: initial language understanding, a performance plateau, and knowledge emergence. They show that the duration of the plateau is influenced by the data distribution, particularly the balance between different individuals (entities). Further, they find that hallucination, defined as overconfident predictions on unseen data, arises concurrently with knowledge acquisition and that fine-tuning on new data is challenging due to the degradation of existing knowledge. Finally, they suggest novel data scheduling strategies.

**Critical Evaluation**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   *Systematic Approach:* The paper adopts a systematic experimental approach, using a synthetic dataset to gain fine-grained control over the training data and enabling a deep dive into learning dynamics.
    *   *Clear Identification of Phases:* The clear demarcation of the three learning phases is a useful contribution, providing a framework for understanding the knowledge acquisition process.
    *   *Data Distribution Insights:* The analysis of how data distribution (specifically imbalances) impacts learning speed and generalization is insightful. The observation that imbalanced distributions can accelerate learning through the plateau phase but also lead to overfitting provides a nuanced understanding.
    *   *Mechanistic Study with Attention Patching:* The attempt to link the plateau phase mechanistically to the formation of attention-based circuits (using attention patching) is a valuable contribution.
    *   *Practical Implications:* The data scheduling strategy (warm-up) offers a practical suggestion for improving LM training.
    *   *Hallucination Analysis:* The observation that hallucination emerges concurrently with knowledge is intriguing, highlighting potential issues with LM calibration.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   *Synthetic Data Limitations:* While the synthetic dataset allows for controlled experiments, it also limits the generalizability of the findings to real-world scenarios. The artificial nature of the biographies may not fully capture the complexities of natural language data and knowledge acquisition.
    *   *Limited Novelty of Concepts:* Some concepts are not entirely new. The observation that data distribution affects learning speed is well-known, although the specific nuances for LMs in factual recall are new. The detrimental impact of fine-tuning on existing knowledge is also a well-documented phenomenon (catastrophic forgetting), though the paper identifies specific factors exacerbating this.
    *   *Mechanistic Explanation Limited:* While attention patching is a good start, the mechanistic explanation of the attention circuit formation during the plateau phase could be more in-depth. The argument that these circuits need to exist *before* learning can continue should be backed up by more solid evidence.
    *   *Overfitting Nuance:* The discussion around overfitting could be more nuanced. While imbalances *can* lead to overfitting, it's not always the case, and the paper could benefit from a more detailed exploration of the conditions under which overfitting occurs in this context.
    *   *Limited Evaluation of Data Scheduling Strategy:* While the warm-up strategy is promising, the paper only presents preliminary results. A more thorough evaluation on a wider range of tasks and datasets would be beneficial.

*   **Significance:**
    *   *Contribution to Understanding LM Learning:* The paper offers a valuable contribution to the growing body of research aimed at understanding how LMs learn and represent knowledge.
    *   *Practical Guidance:* The insights into data distribution and the data scheduling strategy could inform practical LM training practices.
    *   *Hallucination Awareness:* The analysis of hallucination contributes to the ongoing discussion about the limitations of LMs and the need for better methods to ensure their reliability.

*   **Novelty:**
    *   The use of a controlled synthetic dataset to investigate *factual recall* in language models is a valuable methodology, though synthetic datasets are not themselves novel.
    *   The *three-phase* learning dynamic identification, linking it to attention-based circuits, and connecting data imbalances to the length of the plateau offers a novel contribution.
    *   The simultaneous emergence of hallucination and its impact on fine-tuning is a new perspective on the challenges of adding new knowledge.

**Justification for Score:**

Considering the strengths and weaknesses, and focusing on the novelty and potential impact, I assign this paper a score of **7**.

*   The work's systematic approach and clear articulation of the different learning phases are valuable. The insights into the influence of data distribution on learning dynamics are also significant and provide a practical avenue for improving LM training. However, the limitations of the synthetic dataset, the fact that some observations aren't *entirely* new, and the somewhat limited depth of the mechanistic explanation temper the overall score. The work could benefit from more robust evaluation on real-world data and a deeper exploration of the proposed solutions.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### JiraiBench: A Bilingual Benchmark for Evaluating Large Language Models' Detection of Human Self-Destructive Behavior Content in Jirai Community
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.21679v1)
- **Authors**: Yunze Xiao, Tingyu He, Lionel Z. Wang, Yiming Ma, Xingyu Song, Xiaohang Xu, Irene Li, Ka Chung Ng
- **Abstract**: This paper introduces JiraiBench, the first bilingual benchmark for evaluating large language models' effectiveness in detecting self-destructive content across Chinese and Japanese social media communities. Focusing on the transnational "Jirai" (landmine) online subculture that encompasses multiple forms of self-destructive behaviors including drug overdose, eating disorders, and self-harm, we present a comprehensive evaluation framework incorporating both linguistic and cultural dimensions. Our dataset comprises 10,419 Chinese posts and 5,000 Japanese posts with multidimensional annotation along three behavioral categories, achieving substantial inter-annotator agreement. Experimental evaluations across four state-of-the-art models reveal significant performance variations based on instructional language, with Japanese prompts unexpectedly outperforming Chinese prompts when processing Chinese content. This emergent cross-cultural transfer suggests that cultural proximity can sometimes outweigh linguistic similarity in detection tasks. Cross-lingual transfer experiments with fine-tuned models further demonstrate the potential for knowledge transfer between these language systems without explicit target language training. These findings highlight the need for culturally-informed approaches to multilingual content moderation and provide empirical evidence for the importance of cultural context in developing more effective detection systems for vulnerable online communities.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces JiraiBench, a novel bilingual benchmark (Chinese and Japanese) for evaluating the ability of Large Language Models (LLMs) to detect self-destructive behavior content within the "Jirai" online subculture. This subculture encompasses individuals expressing various forms of psychological distress and self-destructive tendencies, including drug overdose, eating disorders, and self-harm. The benchmark consists of a dataset comprising Chinese and Japanese social media posts, annotated across three behavioral categories with high inter-annotator agreement.  The paper presents experiments using state-of-the-art LLMs, highlighting performance variations based on instructional language and demonstrating cross-lingual transfer capabilities through fine-tuning.  A key finding is the unexpected outperformance of Japanese prompts over Chinese prompts when processing Chinese content, suggesting the importance of cultural proximity in detection tasks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The paper's primary novelty lies in the creation of a bilingual benchmark specifically targeting self-destructive behaviors within a distinct online subculture (Jirai).  While prior research has explored mental health detection in social media, the focus on this specific subculture and the cross-lingual aspect represent a valuable contribution. Existing works tend to focus on individual behavior categories in mostly English content, and the new benchmark addresses this gap by capturing the complex comorbidities with multilingualism.
*   **Significance:** The benchmark offers significant practical value for developing culturally-aware and multilingual content moderation systems. The discovery of the Japanese prompt outperforming Chinese prompts on Chinese content is an interesting insight, highlighting the potentially understated role of cultural context relative to linguistic alignment. The exploration of cross-lingual transfer through fine-tuning is also a useful step towards resource-efficient model development.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Well-Defined Problem:** Clearly articulates the limitations of existing approaches and the need for a culturally-sensitive, bilingual benchmark.
    *   **Comprehensive Dataset:**  The dataset size (10,419 Chinese and 5,000 Japanese posts) is reasonably large and is thoroughly annotated, with strong inter-annotator agreement reported.
    *   **Interesting Findings:** The cross-cultural transfer results are intriguing and warrant further investigation. The paper clearly showcases a deviation from traditional expectations.
    *   **Reproducibility:** Clear methodology on how data was collected, the models that were used, and the fine-tuning process.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Model Evaluation:** While four LLMs were tested, a more extensive evaluation with a wider range of models (especially larger, more recent ones) could strengthen the findings. The reasons for not using GPT-4 should have also been explained more thoroughly.
    *   **Cultural Proximity Explanation:**  The explanation for why Japanese prompts outperform Chinese prompts on Chinese data is somewhat speculative. While the cultural origin explanation is plausible, the paper lacks direct evidence to support this. Exploring the nuances of the prompts themselves or specific terminology that is better captured by the Japanese prompt could strengthen this claim.
    *   **Limited Error Analysis:** While macro-F1 is reported, a deeper dive into the specific types of errors the models are making (e.g., false positives, false negatives for specific subcategories) would provide more actionable insights.
    *   **Limited Comparison to SOTA:** The models used were state-of-the-art in 2024 and 2025. Adding a comparison to newer SOTA models will ensure that the results can be replicated.

*   **Potential Influence:**  The JiraiBench benchmark has the potential to stimulate further research in culturally-aware content moderation, particularly for East Asian online communities. The findings could inform the development of more effective early detection systems for vulnerable individuals.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel and valuable benchmark for a complex problem. The experimental results, particularly the cross-cultural transfer findings, offer interesting insights. However, the limited model evaluation, somewhat speculative explanation of the cultural proximity effect, and the absence of more in-depth error analysis hold the paper back from achieving a higher score. Therefore, the score is justified as a solid contribution with room for improvement.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### LLM-Gomoku: A Large Language Model-Based System for Strategic Gomoku with Self-Play and Reinforcement Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.21683v1)
- **Authors**: Hui Wang
- **Abstract**: In recent years, large language models (LLMs) have shown significant advancements in natural language processing (NLP), with strong capa-bilities in generation, comprehension, and rea-soning. These models have found applications in education, intelligent decision-making, and gaming. However, effectively utilizing LLMs for strategic planning and decision-making in the game of Gomoku remains a challenge. This study aims to develop a Gomoku AI system based on LLMs, simulating the human learning process of playing chess. The system is de-signed to understand and apply Gomoku strat-egies and logic to make rational decisions. The research methods include enabling the model to "read the board," "understand the rules," "select strategies," and "evaluate positions," while en-hancing its abilities through self-play and rein-forcement learning. The results demonstrate that this approach significantly improves the se-lection of move positions, resolves the issue of generating illegal positions, and reduces pro-cess time through parallel position evaluation. After extensive self-play training, the model's Gomoku-playing capabilities have been notably enhanced.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "LLM-Gomoku: A Large Language Model-Based System for Strategic Gomoku with Self-Play and Reinforcement Learning."

**Summary:**

The paper presents a system for playing Gomoku (Five-in-a-Row) using Large Language Models (LLMs). The system aims to simulate human strategic thinking in the game by incorporating elements such as understanding game rules, selecting strategies, and evaluating positions. The approach leverages self-play and reinforcement learning to improve the LLM's decision-making capabilities.  Key components include a carefully designed prompt template, a strategy and analytical logic selection process, a local position evaluation method to avoid illegal moves, and a reinforcement learning framework based on Deep Q-Networks to optimize strategy selection. Parallel processing is used to speed up position evaluation. The performance of the system is assessed through qualitative human evaluations and quantitative comparisons against AlphaZero, focusing on the number of steps the LLM agent can survive.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Strengths:**

    * **Integration of LLMs into Strategic Game Playing:** The paper addresses the challenge of effectively utilizing LLMs in complex strategic games like Gomoku. This is a relevant and potentially impactful research direction, given the increasing capabilities of LLMs and their application in various domains.
    * **Human-Mimicking Approach:** The design of the system, particularly the prompt engineering and strategy/logic selection process, explicitly aims to simulate human cognitive processes in game playing. This approach is valuable for developing AI agents that are not only effective but also interpretable.
    * **Addressing Illegal Moves:** The local position evaluation method is a practical solution to a common problem when using LLMs in constrained environments like board games. This demonstrates an awareness of the practical limitations of LLMs and an effort to mitigate them.
    * **Use of Reinforcement Learning:**  The incorporation of reinforcement learning, specifically a Deep Q-Network, allows the system to learn and improve its strategy selection over time through self-play. This is a standard, but essential component for building a competitive game-playing AI.
    * **Parallelization:** The application of parallel processing to position evaluation is a significant optimization that improves the efficiency of the system.
    * **State-Action-Reward Database:** The state-action-reward database to save the process of model training when API calls are interrupted.

* **Weaknesses:**

    * **Limited Novelty in Core Techniques:** While the integration of different techniques is valuable, the individual components (prompt engineering, DQN, self-play) are relatively standard in the fields of NLP and reinforcement learning. The novelty primarily lies in their specific application to Gomoku with LLMs.
    * **Evaluation Limitations:** The evaluation metrics are somewhat limited. Human qualitative assessment is subjective, and survival steps against AlphaZero only provide a limited view of performance. More robust evaluation metrics, such as Elo rating or win rates against a wider range of opponents, would strengthen the paper.  It is also not entirely clear what version of AlphaZero is being used for comparison and what the compute resources were.
    * **Lack of Ablation Studies:** The paper lacks ablation studies to isolate the impact of individual components (e.g., prompt design, local position evaluation) on overall performance. This makes it difficult to assess the relative importance of each element.
    * **Limited Detail on LLM Selection and Prompt Tuning:** The paper mentions the use of LLMs like Deepseek-v2.5, however, there is a lack of detail regarding the specific prompt tuning strategies used to achieve optimal performance with these models. The prompt example given is a little too generic to replicate well without more details.
    * **Overstatement of Results:** The paper claims a "qualitative leap" in Gomoku-playing ability after training, but the data presented (average survival steps) only show a marginal improvement. The statement needs to be more supported by evidence.
    * **Comparison to AlphaZero:** Comparing the system to AlphaZero without detailed specification of the AlphaZero model and training parameters limits the significance of the comparison. AlphaZero is a very powerful system, and a Gomoku variant of AlphaZero would likely significantly outperform this LLM-based approach.

* **Significance:**

    * The paper contributes to the growing body of research on applying LLMs to tasks beyond traditional NLP.
    * It provides practical insights into the challenges and opportunities of using LLMs for strategic decision-making in game environments.
    * It demonstrates the potential for combining LLMs with reinforcement learning to create more capable and adaptive AI agents.
    * Addresses some of the practical issues faced when interfacing LLMs with games, such as dealing with invalid actions.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a reasonable exploration of using LLMs for strategic game playing, combining several established techniques. However, the limited novelty in the core methods, the lack of comprehensive evaluation, and the potential overstatement of results lead me to assign a moderate score. The ideas are not revolutionary, but the system is engineered to overcome practical challenges and the overall research direction is valuable.
